People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hi... yeah, I guess I'm here because I just barely sleep right anymore. It’s like the whole night is just this long, drawn-out ordeal now. I'll just lie there in my bed, staring at the ceiling for hours, and if it's raining—man, even if it's just a little drizzle outside—I hear that rain hitting the window and my brain just goes into overdrive. I start thinking about the bluff, you know? Just picturing the bluff giving way and everything sliding, and I just can't turn my head off no matter what I do. I try to look at my phone or whatever to distract myself, but it doesn't really work. 

And then, when I finally do manage to pass out just from pure exhaustion, it's not even good sleep. I have these dreams, and I'll wake up completely drenched in sweat, like my sheets are just soaked and my heart is pounding. It's happening basically every night, so by the time my alarm actually goes off, I have to drag myself out of bed and show up to my shift at the call center feeling like I never even closed my eyes. And let me tell you, working customer service and having people yell at you on the phone all day when your brain feels like mush because you haven't really slept... it's just getting to be way too much. I can't keep doing it like this.

Well, the dreams... yeah, they're always about the bluff. It's like I'm right back there, you know? But the thing is, it's not even just the dreams anymore. That's what's really starting to mess with me. I'll be wide awake, just sitting at my desk at the call center, right? I've got my headset on, I'm trying to help some lady figure out why her bill is five dollars higher this month, and out of absolutely nowhere—I mean, totally unprovoked—stuff just pops into my head. 

Like, I'll suddenly hear this sound. It's this very specific sound of rocks sliding, that kind of grinding, heavy crunching noise. Or I'll get this sudden, vivid feeling like my leg is stuck under me again, pinned down, and I can't move it. And for a minute... man, for a minute, I'm not in that cubicle anymore. I'm not looking at my computer screen. I'm right back on that trail, feeling the dirt and the panic all over again. It's so real, it takes my breath away, and I have to physically shake my head to snap out of it and remember I'm at work. And the lady on the phone is going, "Hello? Hello? Are you still there?" and I'm just sitting there trying to remember how to breathe. It happens when I'm driving, too, or just watching TV. Just... boom. Right back there.

Well, yeah, actually... I mean, I try really hard to make sure I'm not exposed to certain things, you know? Like, I used to go out to the coast trails all the time. Every weekend, basically. It was my whole thing, just getting out of the city, getting away from the phones and the yelling customers. But now? Man, I won't go anywhere near those trails. I won't even drive on the highway that heads out that direction. If I have to go somewhere and the GPS tries to route me that way, I'll literally take a 45-minute detour just so I don't have to see the signs for the state park. 

And it's not just the places, it's... well, it's the guys I used to go with, too. My hiking buddies. They keep texting me, right? Like, "Hey man, we're hitting the ridge this weekend, you in?" or "Haven't seen you in a while, let's go grab a beer and plan a trip." And I just... I dodge them. I leave them on read. I feel terrible about it, because they're good guys and we've been friends since high school, but even just seeing their names pop up on my phone... it makes my chest completely tighten up. Just the thought of having to talk to them, knowing they're gonna bring up hiking, or ask me why I haven't been out, or—God forbid—talk about the bluff... I just can't do it. My heart starts racing, I can't catch my breath, and I just have to toss my phone across the room. So I just avoid them entirely. I haven't seen any of them in months. It's just easier to stay in my apartment, you know? Where it's safe and there's no rocks and no trails.

I'll be wide awake, just sitting at my desk at the call center, right? I've got my headset on, I'm trying to help some lady figure out why her bill is five dollars higher this month, and out of absolutely nowhere—I mean, totally unprovoked—stuff just pops into my head. \n\nLike, I'll suddenly hear this sound. I'm not looking at my computer screen. I'm right back on that trail, feeling the dirt and the panic all over again. It's so real, it takes my breath away, and I have to physically shake my head to snap out of it and remember I'm at work. And the lady on the phone is going, \"Hello? Hello? Are you still there?\" and I'm just sitting there trying to remember how to breathe. It happens when I'm driving, too, or just watching TV. Just... boom. I mean, I try really hard to make sure I'm not exposed to certain things, you know? Like, I used to go out to the coast trails all the time. Every weekend, basically. It was my whole thing, just getting out of the city, getting away from the phones and the yelling customers. But now? Man, I won't go anywhere near those trails. I won't even drive on the highway that heads out that direction. If I have to go somewhere and the GPS tries to route me that way, I'll literally take a 45-minute detour just so I don't have to see the signs for the state park. \n\nAnd it's not just the places, it's... well, it's the guys I used to go with, too. My hiking buddies. They keep texting me, right? Like, \"Hey man, we're hitting the ridge this weekend, you in?\" or \"Haven't seen you in a while, let's go grab a beer and plan a trip.\" And I just... I dodge them. I leave them on read. I feel terrible about it, because they're good guys and we've been friends since high school, but even just seeing their names pop up on my phone... it makes my chest completely tighten up. Just the thought of having to talk to them, knowing they're gonna bring up hiking, or ask me why I haven't been out, or—God forbid—talk about the bluff... I just can't do it. My heart starts racing, I can't catch my breath, and I just have to toss my phone across the room. So I just avoid them entirely. I haven't seen any of them in months. Yeah, I mean... absolutely. It's not just a little anxiety, it's like this whole full-body reaction, you know? It's like I'm right back there, you know? But the thing is, it's not even just the dreams anymore. That's what's really starting to mess with me. I'll be wide awake, just sitting at my desk at the call center, right? I've got my headset on, I'm trying to help some lady figure out why her bill is five dollars higher this month, and out of absolutely nowhere—I mean, totally unprovoked—stuff just pops into my head. \n\nLike, I'll suddenly hear this sound. It's this very specific sound of rocks sliding, that kind of grinding, heavy crunching noise. Or I'll get this sudden, vivid feeling like my leg is stuck under me again, pinned down, and I can't move it. And for a minute... man, for a minute, I'm not in that cubicle anymore. I'm not looking at my computer screen. I'm right back on that trail, feeling the dirt and the panic all over again. It's so real, it takes my breath away, and I have to physically shake my head to snap out of it and remember I'm at work. And the lady on the phone is going, \"Hello? Hello? Are you still there?\" and I'm just sitting there trying to remember how to breathe. It happens when I'm driving, too, or just watching TV. Just... boom. Right back there.\n\nDoctor: Are these flashbacks or memories of the bluff happening at any specific times or when you're exposed to certain things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually... I mean, I try really hard to make sure I'm not exposed to certain things, you know? Like, I used to go out to the coast trails all the time. Every weekend, basically. It was my whole thing, just getting out of the city, getting away from the phones and the yelling customers. But now? Man, I won't go anywhere near those trails. I won't even drive on the highway that heads out that direction. If I have to go somewhere and the GPS tries to route me that way, I'll literally take a 45-minute detour just so I don't have to see the signs for the state park. \n\nAnd it's not just the places, it's... well, it's the guys I used to go with, too. My hiking buddies. They keep texting me, right? Like, \"Hey man, we're hitting the ridge this weekend, you in?\" or \"Haven't seen you in a while, let's go grab a beer and plan a trip.\" And I just... I dodge them. I leave them on read. I feel terrible about it, because they're good guys and we've been friends since high school, but even just seeing their names pop up on my phone... it makes my chest completely tighten up. Just the thought of having to talk to them, knowing they're gonna bring up hiking, or ask me why I haven't been out, or—God forbid—talk about the bluff... I just can't do it. My heart starts racing, I can't catch my breath, and I just have to toss my phone across the room. So I just avoid them entirely. I haven't seen any of them in months. It's just easier to stay in my apartment, you know? Where it's safe and there's no rocks and no trails.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense fear or anxiety when you think about the bluff or when something reminds you of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... absolutely. It's not just a little anxiety, it's like this whole full-body reaction, you know? Like, my chest gets incredibly tight, almost like someone is sitting on it, and I can't pull in a full breath. And my heart... man, my heart just starts hammering against my ribs so hard I can hear it in my ears. \n\nEven if it's just something small, like seeing a commercial on TV for, I don't know, hiking boots or an SUV driving up a mountain road. I literally have to change the channel or leave the room. It’s like my brain completely short-circuits. I can't focus on anything else. Well, it's been... let's see, it's been about four months now. Yeah, four months. It started pretty much right after it happened. You know, after the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped under all that debris. I mean, at first, when I was in the hospital, I thought maybe I was just shaken up, right? But that's when it really started hitting me. The first time I saw a rock wall on some nature documentary my roommate was watching, and I just completely lost it. And it hasn't stopped since. Every single day for the last four months, just dealing with this constant, exhausting panic every time something even remotely reminds me of being stuck out there. It feels like a lot longer than four months, to be honest with you. It feels like it's been my whole life now, just trying to navigate around all these triggers and memories for a solid four months.

My heart starts racing, I can't catch my breath, and I just have to toss my phone across the room. So I just avoid them entirely. I haven't seen any of them in months. It's just easier to stay in my apartment, you know? I literally have to change the channel or leave the room. It’s like my brain completely short-circuits. I can't focus on anything else. I'll just be sitting on my couch, totally safe, but my body is reacting like I'm about to get crushed all over again. It’s exhausting, honestly, just feeling that level of terror over a thought or a picture.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intense fear and anxiety reactions when reminded of the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... let's see, it's been about four months now. Yeah, four months. It started pretty much right after it happened. You know, after the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped under all that debris. I mean, at first, when I was in the hospital, I thought maybe I was just shaken up, right? Because, I mean, who wouldn't be? But then I got discharged, and I went back to my apartment, and I thought, okay, I'm safe now, I can just get back to normal. But that's when it really started hitting me. The first time I saw a rock wall on some nature documentary my roommate was watching, and I just completely lost it. And it hasn't stopped since. Every single day for the last four months, just dealing with this constant, exhausting panic every time something even remotely reminds me of being stuck out there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I try really, really hard not to think about it. It's like I was saying before, about the trails and my friends, you know? I just completely avoid anything that might bring it up. I won't go near the coast trails now, not even close. Like I said, I'll take a massive detour just to avoid the highway that goes out there. And I dodge texts from my hiking buddies because even talking about going out there, or just seeing their names and knowing they might bring it up... it makes my chest tighten up so bad. 

But it's not just the physical stuff, it's the thoughts, too. If I'm just sitting there and my mind starts to wander, and I feel that memory creeping in... like, the sound of the rocks shifting or the feeling of the dirt, I immediately have to do something else. I'll turn on the TV really loud, or I'll start scrubbing the kitchen counters, or I'll just put on headphones and blast music until my ears ring. Anything to drown it out. Because if I let myself think about it for even a second, it's like I'm right back under that debris, and I just can't handle it. So, yeah, I push it away. I push it away as hard as I can, every single time it pops into my head, because if I don't, I just spiral completely out of control.

I'll just be sitting on my couch, totally safe, but my body is reacting like I'm about to get crushed all over again. It’s exhausting, honestly, just feeling that level of terror over a thought or a picture.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intense fear and anxiety reactions when reminded of the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... let's see, it's been about four months now. Yeah, four months. It started pretty much right after it happened. You know, after the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped under all that debris. I mean, at first, when I was in the hospital, I thought maybe I was just shaken up, right? Because, I mean, who wouldn't be? But then I got discharged, and I went back to my apartment, and I thought, okay, I'm safe now, I can just get back to normal. But that's when it really started hitting me. The first time I saw a rock wall on some nature documentary my roommate was watching, and I just completely lost it. And it hasn't stopped since. Every single day for the last four months, just dealing with this constant, exhausting panic every time something even remotely reminds me of being stuck out there. It feels like a lot longer than four months, to be honest with you. It feels like it's been my whole life now, just trying to navigate around all these triggers and memories for a solid four months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts about the bluff when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I try really, really hard not to think about it. It's like I was saying before, about the trails and my friends, you know? I just completely avoid anything that might bring it up. I won't go near the coast trails now, not even close. Like I said, I'll take a massive detour just to avoid the highway that goes out there. And I dodge texts from my hiking buddies because even talking about going out there, or just seeing their names and knowing they might bring it up... it makes my chest tighten up so bad. \n\nBut it's not just the physical stuff, it's the thoughts, too. If I'm just sitting there and my mind starts to wander, and I feel that memory creeping in... like, the sound of the rocks shifting or the feeling of the dirt, I immediately have to do something else. I'll turn on the TV really loud, or I'll start scrubbing the kitchen counters, or I'll just put on headphones and blast music until my ears ring. Anything to drown it out. Because if I let myself think about it for even a second, it's like I'm right back under that debris, and I just can't handle it. So, yeah, I push it away. Well, I mean... I don't know if I'd say I've lost interest, really. It's more just... I don't know, it's complicated, I guess. Like, I still *want* to do things, you know? I still think about the things I used to like doing. Before all this happened, I was always doing something. Like, I'd go to the movies with my friends, or we'd go grab food downtown, or I'd just spend hours playing video games on the weekends. I still like the idea of those things. I don't think my actual *interest* in them has disappeared, if that makes sense. 

It's just that right now, my brain is so preoccupied with everything else. Like, I'm spending so much energy just trying to keep my head above water, trying not to think about the bluff, trying not to have a panic attack when I see a commercial for a jeep. It takes up a lot of mental space, you know? So when it comes to actually doing the things I used to enjoy, I just... I haven't really been doing them, but not because I don't care about them anymore. It's more like I just don't have the bandwidth right now. I'm too exhausted from just trying to get through a normal day at work, dealing with customers and trying to act like I'm totally fine when inside my chest is doing that tight thing I told you about. So by the time I get home, I just kind of crash. But if you're asking if I just don't like those things anymore, or if they don't seem fun to me... no, I wouldn't say that. I just haven't really had the chance to focus on them lately, with everything else going on. I'm sure if things settle down, I'll get back into all of it. I just... I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those things, just how much I actually *do* them right now.

I start thinking about the bluff, you know? Just picturing the bluff giving way and everything sliding, and I just can't turn my head off no matter what I do. I try to look at my phone or whatever to distract myself, but it doesn't really work. \n\nAnd then, when I finally do manage to pass out just from pure exhaustion, it's not even good sleep. I have these dreams, and I'll wake up completely drenched in sweat, like my sheets are just soaked and my heart is pounding. It's happening basically every night, so by the time my alarm actually goes off, I have to drag myself out of bed and show up to my shift at the call center feeling like I never even closed my eyes. And let me tell you, working customer service and having people yell at you on the phone all day when your brain feels like mush because you haven't really slept... it's just getting to be way too much. I can't keep doing it like this.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having dreams that leave you drenched in sweat, can you tell me more about what happens in those dreams?\n\nPatient: Well, the dreams... yeah, they're always about the bluff. It's like I'm right back there, you know? But the thing is, it's not even just the dreams anymore. That's what's really starting to mess with me. I'll be wide awake, just sitting at my desk at the call center, right? I've got my headset on, I'm trying to help some lady figure out why her bill is five dollars higher this month, and out of absolutely nowhere—I mean, totally unprovoked—stuff just pops into my head. \n\nLike, I'll suddenly hear this sound. It's this very specific sound of rocks sliding, that kind of grinding, heavy crunching noise. Or I'll get this sudden, vivid feeling like my leg is stuck under me again, pinned down, and I can't move it. And for a minute... man, for a minute, I'm not in that cubicle anymore. I'm not looking at my computer screen. I'm right back on that trail, feeling the dirt and the panic all over again. It's so real, it takes my breath away, and I have to physically shake my head to snap out of it and remember I'm at work. And the lady on the phone is going, \"Hello? Hello? Are you still there?\" and I'm just sitting there trying to remember how to breathe. It happens when I'm driving, too, or just watching TV. Just... boom. Right back there.\n\nDoctor: Are these flashbacks or memories of the bluff happening at any specific times or when you're exposed to certain things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually... I mean, I try really hard to make sure I'm not exposed to certain things, you know? Like, I used to go out to the coast trails all the time. Every weekend, basically. It was my whole thing, just getting out of the city, getting away from the phones and the yelling customers. But now? Man, I won't go anywhere near those trails. I won't even drive on the highway that heads out that direction. If I have to go somewhere and the GPS tries to route me that way, I'll literally take a 45-minute detour just so I don't have to see the signs for the state park. \n\nAnd it's not just the places, it's... well, it's the guys I used to go with, too. My hiking buddies. They keep texting me, right? Like, \"Hey man, we're hitting the ridge this weekend, you in?\" or \"Haven't seen you in a while, let's go grab a beer and plan a trip.\" And I just... I dodge them. I leave them on read. I feel terrible about it, because they're good guys and we've been friends since high school, but even just seeing their names pop up on my phone... it makes my chest completely tighten up. Just the thought of having to talk to them, knowing they're gonna bring up hiking, or ask me why I haven't been out, or—God forbid—talk about the bluff... I just can't do it. My heart starts racing, I can't catch my breath, and I just have to toss my phone across the room. So I just avoid them entirely. I haven't seen any of them in months. It's just easier to stay in my apartment, you know? Where it's safe and there's no rocks and no trails.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense fear or anxiety when you think about the bluff or when something reminds you of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... absolutely. It's not just a little anxiety, it's like this whole full-body reaction, you know? Like, my chest gets incredibly tight, almost like someone is sitting on it, and I can't pull in a full breath. And my heart... man, my heart just starts hammering against my ribs so hard I can hear it in my ears. \n\nEven if it's just something small, like seeing a commercial on TV for, I don't know, hiking boots or an SUV driving up a mountain road. Just the sight of a rocky cliff on a screen, and suddenly I'm sweating, my hands get all clammy, and I feel this overwhelming panic, like I need to run away right that second. I literally have to change the channel or leave the room. It’s like my brain completely short-circuits. I can't focus on anything else. I'll just be sitting on my couch, totally safe, but my body is reacting like I'm about to get crushed all over again. It’s exhausting, honestly, just feeling that level of terror over a thought or a picture.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intense fear and anxiety reactions when reminded of the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... let's see, it's been about four months now. Yeah, four months. It started pretty much right after it happened. You know, after the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped under all that debris. I mean, at first, when I was in the hospital, I thought maybe I was just shaken up, right? Because, I mean, who wouldn't be? But then I got discharged, and I went back to my apartment, and I thought, okay, I'm safe now, I can just get back to normal. But that's when it really started hitting me. The first time I saw a rock wall on some nature documentary my roommate was watching, and I just completely lost it. And it hasn't stopped since. Every single day for the last four months, just dealing with this constant, exhausting panic every time something even remotely reminds me of being stuck out there. It feels like a lot longer than four months, to be honest with you. It feels like it's been my whole life now, just trying to navigate around all these triggers and memories for a solid four months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts about the bluff when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I try really, really hard not to think about it. It's like I was saying before, about the trails and my friends, you know? I just completely avoid anything that might bring it up. I won't go near the coast trails now, not even close. Like I said, I'll take a massive detour just to avoid the highway that goes out there. And I dodge texts from my hiking buddies because even talking about going out there, or just seeing their names and knowing they might bring it up... it makes my chest tighten up so bad. \n\nBut it's not just the physical stuff, it's the thoughts, too. If I'm just sitting there and my mind starts to wander, and I feel that memory creeping in... like, the sound of the rocks shifting or the feeling of the dirt, I immediately have to do something else. I'll turn on the TV really loud, or I'll start scrubbing the kitchen counters, or I'll just put on headphones and blast music until my ears ring. Anything to drown it out. Because if I let myself think about it for even a second, it's like I'm right back under that debris, and I just can't handle it. So, yeah, I push it away. I push it away as hard as I can, every single time it pops into my head, because if I don't, I just spiral completely out of control.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy before the incident at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I don't know if I'd say I've lost interest, really. It's more just... I don't know, it's complicated, I guess. Like, I still *want* to do things, you know? I still think about the things I used to like doing. Before all this happened, I was always doing something. Like, I'd go to the movies with my friends, or we'd go grab food downtown, or I'd just spend hours playing video games on the weekends. I still like the idea of those things. I don't think my actual *interest* in them has disappeared, if that makes sense. \n\nIt's just that right now, my brain is so preoccupied with everything else. Like, I'm spending so much energy just trying to keep my head above water, trying not to think about the bluff, trying not to have a panic attack when I see a commercial for a jeep. It takes up a lot of mental space, you know? So when it comes to actually doing the things I used to enjoy, I just... I haven't really been doing them, but not because I don't care about them anymore. It's more like I just don't have the bandwidth right now. I'm too exhausted from just trying to get through a normal day at work, dealing with customers and trying to act like I'm totally fine when inside my chest is doing that tight thing I told you about. So by the time I get home, I just kind of crash. But if you're asking if I just don't like those things anymore, or if they don't seem fun to me... no, I wouldn't say that. I just haven't really had the chance to focus on them lately, with everything else going on. I'm sure if things settle down, I'll get back into all of it. I just... I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those things, just how much I actually *do* them right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Posttraumatic Stress Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intense fear and anxiety reactions when reminded of the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... let's see, it's been about four months now. Yeah, four months. It started pretty much right after it happened. You know, after the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped under all that debris. I mean, at first, when I was in the hospital, I thought maybe I was just shaken up, right? Because, I mean, who wouldn't be? But then I got discharged, and I went back to my apartment, and I thought, okay, I'm safe now, I can just get back to normal. But that's when it really started hitting me. The first time I saw a rock wall on some nature documentary my roommate was watching, and I just completely lost it. And it hasn't stopped since. Every single day for the last four months, just dealing with this constant, exhausting panic every time something even remotely reminds me of being stuck out there. It feels like a lot longer than four months, to be honest with you. It feels like it's been my whole life now, just trying to navigate around all these triggers and memories for a solid four months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts about the bluff when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I try really, really hard not to think about it. It's like I was saying before, about the trails and my friends, you know? I just completely avoid anything that might bring it up. I won't go near the coast trails now, not even close. Like I said, I'll take a massive detour just to avoid the highway that goes out there. And I dodge texts from my hiking buddies because even talking about going out there, or just seeing their names and knowing they might bring it up... it makes my chest tighten up so bad. \n\nBut it's not just the physical stuff, it's the thoughts, too. If I'm just sitting there and my mind starts to wander, and I feel that memory creeping in... like, the sound of the rocks shifting or the feeling of the dirt, I immediately have to do something else. I'll turn on the TV really loud, or I'll start scrubbing the kitchen counters, or I'll just put on headphones and blast music until my ears ring. Anything to drown it out. Because if I let myself think about it for even a second, it's like I'm right back under that debris, and I just can't handle it. So, yeah, I push it away. I push it away as hard as I can, every single time it pops into my head, because if I don't, I just spiral completely out of control.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy before the incident at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I don't know if I'd say I've lost interest, really. It's more just... I don't know, it's complicated, I guess. Like, I still *want* to do things, you know? I still think about the things I used to like doing. Before all this happened, I was always doing something. Like, I'd go to the movies with my friends, or we'd go grab food downtown, or I'd just spend hours playing video games on the weekends. I still like the idea of those things. I don't think my actual *interest* in them has disappeared, if that makes sense. \n\nIt's just that right now, my brain is so preoccupied with everything else. Like, I'm spending so much energy just trying to keep my head above water, trying not to think about the bluff, trying not to have a panic attack when I see a commercial for a jeep. It takes up a lot of mental space, you know? So when it comes to actually doing the things I used to enjoy, I just... I haven't really been doing them, but not because I don't care about them anymore. It's more like I just don't have the bandwidth right now. I'm too exhausted from just trying to get through a normal day at work, dealing with customers and trying to act like I'm totally fine when inside my chest is doing that tight thing I told you about. So by the time I get home, I just kind of crash. But if you're asking if I just don't like those things anymore, or if they don't seem fun to me... no, I wouldn't say that. I just haven't really had the chance to focus on them lately, with everything else going on. I'm sure if things settle down, I'll get back into all of it. I just... I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those things, just how much I actually *do* them right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have trouble remembering certain parts of what happened at the bluff?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 21 / customer_service_representative / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, honestly... no. I mean, I really, really wish I did. I wish there were parts I couldn't remember, you know? Because... well, it's actually the exact opposite. I remember every single second of it. It's like it's completely burned into my brain. I remember exactly where I put my foot right before the edge gave way. I remember the exact sound it made—it wasn't like a loud crack or anything, it was this weird, deep shifting noise, like a groan almost, right underneath my boots. 

And I remember the smell of the dust when it all came down on top of me. It smelled like... I don't know, like old dirt and salt water mixed together. I remember the exact weight of the debris on my leg, and the color of the sky right before the dust cloud blocked it out. It was this really bright, clear blue, you know, one of those perfect afternoons. I could probably tell you exactly how many rocks were pinning me down if I sat down and drew it out. 

So, no, I don't have any blank spots at all. If anything, my brain just recorded it in, like, ultra-high definition. And it's frustrating because I watch these movies or hear stories about people who go through something crazy and they just black out and wake up in the hospital, and they don't remember the scary part. And I just think, man, how lucky is that? Because I remember every single terrifying second of being trapped there, just waiting and hoping someone would hear me yelling. Every detail, every minute. There's absolutely nothing missing from that day in my head, unfortunately. I remember it all perfectly.

My hiking buddies. They keep texting me, right? Just the thought of having to talk to them, knowing they're gonna bring up hiking, or ask me why I haven't been out, or—God forbid—talk about the bluff... I just can't do it. My heart starts racing, I can't catch my breath, and I just have to toss my phone across the room. So I just avoid them entirely. I haven't seen any of them in months. It's just easier to stay in my apartment, you know? I literally have to change the channel or leave the room. It’s like my brain completely short-circuits. I can't focus on anything else. I'll just be sitting on my couch, totally safe, but my body is reacting like I'm about to get crushed all over again. It’s exhausting, honestly, just feeling that level of terror over a thought or a picture.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intense fear and anxiety reactions when reminded of the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... let's see, it's been about four months now. Well, yeah, I do. I mean, it's hard to admit, but... yeah. It's not just a passing thought, either. It's this constant, heavy feeling that just sits with me almost every single day. Under the surface, I just feel... scared. Scared and really, really embarrassed. 

You know, before all this happened, I was always the guy who pushed things to the limit. I was the one leading the hikes, the one who wasn't afraid to get close to the edge, the one who acted like nothing could touch me. I had this whole image, I guess. I was tough. I didn't get rattled. And now... now I'm the guy who got trapped under a rockslide because I was stupid enough to stand on an unstable overhang. I should have known better. I grew up around here, I know how the cliffs are after heavy rain, but I just... I didn't think it would happen to me. 

And now, when I'm at work, dealing with customers or whatever, or even just walking down the street, I have this paranoid feeling that everyone can see it on me. Like they can look at me and just tell that I messed up, that I'm weak, that I'm not the tough guy I always pretended to be. It's like I'm wearing a sign that says "I made a huge mistake and now I'm terrified all the time." It's humiliating. I feel like my friends probably talk about it behind my back, too. Like, "Oh, look at him now, can't even drive past a trail without hyperventilating." I just feel so much shame about the whole thing, like I brought it all on myself and now everyone knows I'm a fraud. It just... it eats at me, you know? Most days, that's just the baseline of how I feel. Scared and totally embarrassed.

Every single day for the last four months, just dealing with this constant, exhausting panic every time something even remotely reminds me of being stuck out there. Because if I let myself think about it for even a second, it's like I'm right back under that debris, and I just can't handle it. So, yeah, I push it away. I don't know if I'd say I've lost interest, really. It's more just... I don't know, it's complicated, I guess. Like, I still *want* to do things, you know? I still think about the things I used to like doing. Before all this happened, I was always doing something. Like, I'd go to the movies with my friends, or we'd go grab food downtown, or I'd just spend hours playing video games on the weekends. I still like the idea of those things. I don't think my actual *interest* in them has disappeared, if that makes sense. \n\nIt's just that right now, my brain is so preoccupied with everything else. Like, I'm spending so much energy just trying to keep my head above water, trying not to think about the bluff, trying not to have a panic attack when I see a commercial for a jeep. It takes up a lot of mental space, you know? So when it comes to actually doing the things I used to enjoy, I just... I haven't really been doing them, but not because I don't care about them anymore. It's more like I just don't have the bandwidth right now. It was this really bright, clear blue, you know, one of those perfect afternoons. I could probably tell you exactly how many rocks were pinning me down if I sat down and drew it out. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any blank spots at all. If anything, my brain just recorded it in, like, ultra-high definition. I still think about the things I used to like doing. Before all this happened, I was always doing something. I don't think my actual *interest* in them has disappeared, if that makes sense. \n\nIt's just that right now, my brain is so preoccupied with everything else. Like, I'm spending so much energy just trying to keep my head above water, trying not to think about the bluff, trying not to have a panic attack when I see a commercial for a jeep. It takes up a lot of mental space, you know? So when it comes to actually doing the things I used to enjoy, I just... I just haven't really had the chance to focus on them lately, with everything else going on. I'm sure if things settle down, I'll get back into all of it. I just... I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those things, just how much I actually *do* them right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering certain parts of what happened at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly... no. I mean, I really, really wish I did. I wish there were parts I couldn't remember, you know? Because... well, it's actually the exact opposite. I remember every single second of it. It's like it's completely burned into my brain. I remember exactly where I put my foot right before the edge gave way. I remember the exact sound it made—it wasn't like a loud crack or anything, it was this weird, deep shifting noise, like a groan almost, right underneath my boots. \n\nAnd I remember the smell of the dust when it all came down on top of me. It smelled like... I don't know, like old dirt and salt water mixed together. I remember the exact weight of the debris on my leg, and the color of the sky right before the dust cloud blocked it out. It was this really bright, clear blue, you know, one of those perfect afternoons. I could probably tell you exactly how many rocks were pinning me down if I sat down and drew it out. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any blank spots at all. If anything, my brain just recorded it in, like, ultra-high definition. And it's frustrating because I watch these movies or hear stories about people who go through something crazy and they just black out and wake up in the hospital, and they don't remember the scary part. And I just think, man, how lucky is that? Because I remember every single terrifying second of being trapped there, just waiting and hoping someone would hear me yelling. Every detail, every minute. There's absolutely nothing missing from that day in my head, unfortunately. I remember it all perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about what happened at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do. I mean, it's hard to admit, but... yeah. It's not just a passing thought, either. It's this constant, heavy feeling that just sits with me almost every single day. Under the surface, I just feel... scared. Scared and really, really embarrassed. \n\nYou know, before all this happened, I was always the guy who pushed things to the limit. I was the one leading the hikes, the one who wasn't afraid to get close to the edge, the one who acted like nothing could touch me. I had this whole image, I guess. I was tough. I didn't get rattled. And now... now I'm the guy who got trapped under a rockslide because I was stupid enough to stand on an unstable overhang. I should have known better. I grew up around here, I know how the cliffs are after heavy rain, but I just... I didn't think it would happen to me. \n\nAnd now, when I'm at work, dealing with customers or whatever, or even just walking down the street, I have this paranoid feeling that everyone can see it on me. Like they can look at me and just tell that I messed up, that I'm weak, that I'm not the tough guy I always pretended to be. It's like I'm wearing a sign that says \"I made a huge mistake and now I'm terrified all the time.\" It's humiliating. I feel like my friends probably talk about it behind my back, too. Like, \"Oh, look at him now, can't even drive past a trail without hyperventilating.\" I just feel so much shame about the whole thing, like I brought it all on myself and now everyone knows I'm a fraud. It just... it eats at me, you know? Most days, that's just the baseline of how I feel. Scared and totally embarrassed.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you try to avoid feeling emotions related to what happened at the bluff?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 21 / customer_service_representative / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Trauma_Avoidance\nmanifestation: \"I won't go near the coast trails now, and I dodge texts from my hiking buddies because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, absolutely. I mean, I try to avoid anything that even remotely brings up those feelings, because it's just... it's too much. It's not just the feelings, though, it's the places, the people, everything connected to it. Like, I completely refuse to go anywhere near the coast trails now. I used to go out there all the time, right? Like, at least twice a week, rain or shine. But now, just the thought of driving down that stretch of the highway, seeing the ocean on one side and the cliffs on the other... my stomach just drops. I can't do it. I've been taking this ridiculously long detour to get to work just so I don't have to see the water or the bluffs in the distance. It adds like twenty minutes to my commute, but I don't care. It's better than feeling that panic start to rise up in my throat.

And it's not just the places, it's my friends, too. My hiking buddies... we used to be so tight. We had this group chat where we'd plan all our weekend trips, talk about gear, you know, just normal stuff. But now, I just... I dodge their texts. I leave them on read, or I make up some excuse about picking up extra shifts at work. Because even just texting about going out there, even just seeing a message like, "Hey, weather's looking good for a hike this weekend," it makes my chest tighten up so bad I feel like I can't breathe. My heart starts pounding, and I get that same awful, trapped feeling I had under the rocks. So I just avoid them. I feel terrible about it, because they're good guys and they're probably wondering what's wrong with me, but I just can't handle the emotions that come with even talking about it. So yeah, I try to avoid the feelings, the places, the people... basically anything that reminds me of that day, I just push it as far away as I possibly can.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi... yeah, I guess I'm here because I just barely sleep right anymore. It’s like the whole night is just this long, drawn-out ordeal now. I start thinking about the bluff, you know? Just picturing the bluff giving way and everything sliding, and I just can't turn my head off no matter what I do. I try to look at my phone or whatever to distract myself, but it doesn't really work. \n\nAnd then, when I finally do manage to pass out just from pure exhaustion, it's not even good sleep. I have these dreams, and I'll wake up completely drenched in sweat, like my sheets are just soaked and my heart is pounding. Are you still there?\" and I'm just sitting there trying to remember how to breathe. It happens when I'm driving, too, or just watching TV. Just... boom. Right back there.\n\nDoctor: Are these flashbacks or memories of the bluff happening at any specific times or when you're exposed to certain things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually... My hiking buddies. They keep texting me, right? Like, \"Hey man, we're hitting the ridge this weekend, you in?\" or \"Haven't seen you in a while, let's go grab a beer and plan a trip.\" And I just... I dodge them. I leave them on read. I feel terrible about it, because they're good guys and we've been friends since high school, but even just seeing their names pop up on my phone... it makes my chest completely tighten up. Just the thought of having to talk to them, knowing they're gonna bring up hiking, or ask me why I haven't been out, or—God forbid—talk about the bluff... I just can't do it. My heart starts racing, I can't catch my breath, and I just have to toss my phone across the room. So I just avoid them entirely. I haven't seen any of them in months. It's just easier to stay in my apartment, you know? Where it's safe and there's no rocks and no trails.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense fear or anxiety when you think about the bluff or when something reminds you of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... absolutely. It's not just a little anxiety, it's like this whole full-body reaction, you know? Like, my chest gets incredibly tight, almost like someone is sitting on it, and I can't pull in a full breath. And my heart... man, my heart just starts hammering against my ribs so hard I can hear it in my ears. \n\nEven if it's just something small, like seeing a commercial on TV for, I don't know, hiking boots or an SUV driving up a mountain road. Just the sight of a rocky cliff on a screen, and suddenly I'm sweating, my hands get all clammy, and I feel this overwhelming panic, like I need to run away right that second. I literally have to change the channel or leave the room. It’s like my brain completely short-circuits. I can't focus on anything else. I'll just be sitting on my couch, totally safe, but my body is reacting like I'm about to get crushed all over again. It’s exhausting, honestly, just feeling that level of terror over a thought or a picture.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intense fear and anxiety reactions when reminded of the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... let's see, it's been about four months now. Yeah, four months. It started pretty much right after it happened. You know, after the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped under all that debris. I mean, at first, when I was in the hospital, I thought maybe I was just shaken up, right? Because, I mean, who wouldn't be? But then I got discharged, and I went back to my apartment, and I thought, okay, I'm safe now, I can just get back to normal. But that's when it really started hitting me. The first time I saw a rock wall on some nature documentary my roommate was watching, and I just completely lost it. And it hasn't stopped since. Every single day for the last four months, just dealing with this constant, exhausting panic every time something even remotely reminds me of being stuck out there. It feels like a lot longer than four months, to be honest with you. Where it's safe and there's no rocks and no trails.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense fear or anxiety when you think about the bluff or when something reminds you of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... absolutely. It's not just a little anxiety, it's like this whole full-body reaction, you know? Like, my chest gets incredibly tight, almost like someone is sitting on it, and I can't pull in a full breath. And my heart... man, my heart just starts hammering against my ribs so hard I can hear it in my ears. \n\nEven if it's just something small, like seeing a commercial on TV for, I don't know, hiking boots or an SUV driving up a mountain road. Just the sight of a rocky cliff on a screen, and suddenly I'm sweating, my hands get all clammy, and I feel this overwhelming panic, like I need to run away right that second. I literally have to change the channel or leave the room. It’s like my brain completely short-circuits. I can't focus on anything else. I'll just be sitting on my couch, totally safe, but my body is reacting like I'm about to get crushed all over again. It’s exhausting, honestly, just feeling that level of terror over a thought or a picture.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intense fear and anxiety reactions when reminded of the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... let's see, it's been about four months now. Yeah, four months. It started pretty much right after it happened. You know, after the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped under all that debris. I mean, at first, when I was in the hospital, I thought maybe I was just shaken up, right? Because, I mean, who wouldn't be? But then I got discharged, and I went back to my apartment, and I thought, okay, I'm safe now, I can just get back to normal. But that's when it really started hitting me. The first time I saw a rock wall on some nature documentary my roommate was watching, and I just completely lost it. And it hasn't stopped since. Every single day for the last four months, just dealing with this constant, exhausting panic every time something even remotely reminds me of being stuck out there. It feels like a lot longer than four months, to be honest with you. It feels like it's been my whole life now, just trying to navigate around all these triggers and memories for a solid four months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts about the bluff when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I try really, really hard not to think about it. It's like I was saying before, about the trails and my friends, you know? I just completely avoid anything that might bring it up. I won't go near the coast trails now, not even close. Like I said, I'll take a massive detour just to avoid the highway that goes out there. And I dodge texts from my hiking buddies because even talking about going out there, or just seeing their names and knowing they might bring it up... it makes my chest tighten up so bad. \n\nBut it's not just the physical stuff, it's the thoughts, too. If I'm just sitting there and my mind starts to wander, and I feel that memory creeping in... like, the sound of the rocks shifting or the feeling of the dirt, I immediately have to do something else. I'll turn on the TV really loud, or I'll start scrubbing the kitchen counters, or I'll just put on headphones and blast music until my ears ring. Anything to drown it out. Because if I let myself think about it for even a second, it's like I'm right back under that debris, and I just can't handle it. So, yeah, I push it away. I push it away as hard as I can, every single time it pops into my head, because if I don't, I just spiral completely out of control.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy before the incident at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I don't know if I'd say I've lost interest, really. It's more just... I don't know, it's complicated, I guess. Like, I still *want* to do things, you know? I still think about the things I used to like doing. Before all this happened, I was always doing something. Like, I'd go to the movies with my friends, or we'd go grab food downtown, or I'd just spend hours playing video games on the weekends. I still like the idea of those things. I don't think my actual *interest* in them has disappeared, if that makes sense. \n\nIt's just that right now, my brain is so preoccupied with everything else. Like, I'm spending so much energy just trying to keep my head above water, trying not to think about the bluff, trying not to have a panic attack when I see a commercial for a jeep. It takes up a lot of mental space, you know? So when it comes to actually doing the things I used to enjoy, I just... I haven't really been doing them, but not because I don't care about them anymore. It's more like I just don't have the bandwidth right now. I'm too exhausted from just trying to get through a normal day at work, dealing with customers and trying to act like I'm totally fine when inside my chest is doing that tight thing I told you about. So by the time I get home, I just kind of crash. But if you're asking if I just don't like those things anymore, or if they don't seem fun to me... no, I wouldn't say that. I just haven't really had the chance to focus on them lately, with everything else going on. I'm sure if things settle down, I'll get back into all of it. I just... I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those things, just how much I actually *do* them right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering certain parts of what happened at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly... no. I mean, I really, really wish I did. I wish there were parts I couldn't remember, you know? Because... well, it's actually the exact opposite. I remember every single second of it. It's like it's completely burned into my brain. I remember exactly where I put my foot right before the edge gave way. I remember the exact sound it made—it wasn't like a loud crack or anything, it was this weird, deep shifting noise, like a groan almost, right underneath my boots. \n\nAnd I remember the smell of the dust when it all came down on top of me. It smelled like... I don't know, like old dirt and salt water mixed together. I remember the exact weight of the debris on my leg, and the color of the sky right before the dust cloud blocked it out. It was this really bright, clear blue, you know, one of those perfect afternoons. I could probably tell you exactly how many rocks were pinning me down if I sat down and drew it out. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any blank spots at all. If anything, my brain just recorded it in, like, ultra-high definition. And it's frustrating because I watch these movies or hear stories about people who go through something crazy and they just black out and wake up in the hospital, and they don't remember the scary part. And I just think, man, how lucky is that? Because I remember every single terrifying second of being trapped there, just waiting and hoping someone would hear me yelling. Every detail, every minute. There's absolutely nothing missing from that day in my head, unfortunately. I remember it all perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about what happened at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do. I mean, it's hard to admit, but... yeah. It's not just a passing thought, either. It's this constant, heavy feeling that just sits with me almost every single day. Under the surface, I just feel... scared. Scared and really, really embarrassed. \n\nYou know, before all this happened, I was always the guy who pushed things to the limit. I was the one leading the hikes, the one who wasn't afraid to get close to the edge, the one who acted like nothing could touch me. I had this whole image, I guess. I was tough. I didn't get rattled. And now... now I'm the guy who got trapped under a rockslide because I was stupid enough to stand on an unstable overhang. I should have known better. I grew up around here, I know how the cliffs are after heavy rain, but I just... I didn't think it would happen to me. \n\nAnd now, when I'm at work, dealing with customers or whatever, or even just walking down the street, I have this paranoid feeling that everyone can see it on me. Like they can look at me and just tell that I messed up, that I'm weak, that I'm not the tough guy I always pretended to be. It's like I'm wearing a sign that says \"I made a huge mistake and now I'm terrified all the time.\" It's humiliating. I feel like my friends probably talk about it behind my back, too. Like, \"Oh, look at him now, can't even drive past a trail without hyperventilating.\" I just feel so much shame about the whole thing, like I brought it all on myself and now everyone knows I'm a fraud. It just... it eats at me, you know? Most days, that's just the baseline of how I feel. Scared and totally embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid feeling emotions related to what happened at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, absolutely. I mean, I try to avoid anything that even remotely brings up those feelings, because it's just... it's too much. It's not just the feelings, though, it's the places, the people, everything connected to it. Like, I completely refuse to go anywhere near the coast trails now. I used to go out there all the time, right? Like, at least twice a week, rain or shine. But now, just the thought of driving down that stretch of the highway, seeing the ocean on one side and the cliffs on the other... my stomach just drops. I can't do it. I've been taking this ridiculously long detour to get to work just so I don't have to see the water or the bluffs in the distance. It adds like twenty minutes to my commute, but I don't care. It's better than feeling that panic start to rise up in my throat.\n\nAnd it's not just the places, it's my friends, too. My hiking buddies... we used to be so tight. We had this group chat where we'd plan all our weekend trips, talk about gear, you know, just normal stuff. But now, I just... I dodge their texts. I leave them on read, or I make up some excuse about picking up extra shifts at work. Because even just texting about going out there, even just seeing a message like, \"Hey, weather's looking good for a hike this weekend,\" it makes my chest tighten up so bad I feel like I can't breathe. My heart starts pounding, and I get that same awful, trapped feeling I had under the rocks. So I just avoid them. I feel terrible about it, because they're good guys and they're probably wondering what's wrong with me, but I just can't handle the emotions that come with even talking about it. So yeah, I try to avoid the feelings, the places, the people... basically anything that reminds me of that day, I just push it as far away as I possibly can.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, no, not really. I mean, the hiking, obviously, I don't do that anymore, like I said. But everything else? No, I still like doing the things I always liked doing. Like, I'm a big gamer, right? I've got this whole setup in my living room, dual monitors, the whole deal. I play a lot of these open-world RPGs, and honestly, I probably play them even more now than I used to. It's a good distraction, you know? I can just put my headset on, get lost in this totally different world for hours, and not have to think about anything else. I'll sit down after a shift at work, and next thing I know, it's 2:00 in the morning and I've just been grinding out quests the whole time. 

And I still like working on my car. I've got this old Honda Civic that I've been slowly fixing up since high school. It's nothing fancy, but I like tinkering with it. Just last weekend, I spent like four hours in the driveway changing out the brake pads and flushing the fluids. It's satisfying, you know, getting your hands dirty and actually fixing a problem you can see and touch. It's straightforward. So yeah, I still enjoy that just as much as I always did. 

And I still watch movies, I still listen to the same music... I don't know, I guess my interests haven't really changed at all. It's just the outdoor stuff, the stuff that involves being near the cliffs or out in nature, that's completely gone. But the inside stuff, the stuff I do at home? That's all still there. If anything, I lean into those hobbies a lot harder now because it's safe. I know exactly what to expect when I boot up a game or pop the hood of my car. There's no surprises. So, no, I haven't lost interest in my hobbies or anything like that. I just... I just stick to the ones that keep me indoors, I guess.

I'll just be sitting on my couch, totally safe, but my body is reacting like I'm about to get crushed all over again. It’s exhausting, honestly, just feeling that level of terror over a thought or a picture.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intense fear and anxiety reactions when reminded of the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... let's see, it's been about four months now. Yeah, four months. It started pretty much right after it happened. You know, after the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped under all that debris. I mean, at first, when I was in the hospital, I thought maybe I was just shaken up, right? Because, I mean, who wouldn't be? But then I got discharged, and I went back to my apartment, and I thought, okay, I'm safe now, I can just get back to normal. But that's when it really started hitting me. The first time I saw a rock wall on some nature documentary my roommate was watching, and I just completely lost it. And it hasn't stopped since. Every single day for the last four months, just dealing with this constant, exhausting panic every time something even remotely reminds me of being stuck out there. It feels like a lot longer than four months, to be honest with you. It feels like it's been my whole life now, just trying to navigate around all these triggers and memories for a solid four months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts about the bluff when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I try really, really hard not to think about it. It's like I was saying before, about the trails and my friends, you know? I just completely avoid anything that might bring it up. I won't go near the coast trails now, not even close. Like I said, I'll take a massive detour just to avoid the highway that goes out there. I still think about the things I used to like doing. Before all this happened, I was always doing something. I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those things, just how much I actually *do* them right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering certain parts of what happened at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly... no. It's like it's completely burned into my brain. I remember exactly where I put my foot right before the edge gave way. I remember the exact sound it made—it wasn't like a loud crack or anything, it was this weird, deep shifting noise, like a groan almost, right underneath my boots. \n\nAnd I remember the smell of the dust when it all came down on top of me. Scared and really, really embarrassed. \n\nYou know, before all this happened, I was always the guy who pushed things to the limit. I was the one leading the hikes, the one who wasn't afraid to get close to the edge, the one who acted like nothing could touch me. I had this whole image, I guess. I was tough. I didn't get rattled. And now... now I'm the guy who got trapped under a rockslide because I was stupid enough to stand on an unstable overhang. I should have known better. I grew up around here, I know how the cliffs are after heavy rain, but I just... I didn't think it would happen to me. \n\nAnd now, when I'm at work, dealing with customers or whatever, or even just walking down the street, I have this paranoid feeling that everyone can see it on me. Like they can look at me and just tell that I messed up, that I'm weak, that I'm not the tough guy I always pretended to be. It's like I'm wearing a sign that says \"I made a huge mistake and now I'm terrified all the time.\" It's humiliating. I feel like my friends probably talk about it behind my back, too. Like, \"Oh, look at him now, can't even drive past a trail without hyperventilating.\" I just feel so much shame about the whole thing, like I brought it all on myself and now everyone knows I'm a fraud. It just... it eats at me, you know? Most days, that's just the baseline of how I feel. Scared and totally embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid feeling emotions related to what happened at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, absolutely. I mean, I try to avoid anything that even remotely brings up those feelings, because it's just... it's too much. It's not just the feelings, though, it's the places, the people, everything connected to it. Like, I completely refuse to go anywhere near the coast trails now. I used to go out there all the time, right? Like, at least twice a week, rain or shine. But now, just the thought of driving down that stretch of the highway, seeing the ocean on one side and the cliffs on the other... my stomach just drops. I can't do it. I've been taking this ridiculously long detour to get to work just so I don't have to see the water or the bluffs in the distance. It adds like twenty minutes to my commute, but I don't care. It's better than feeling that panic start to rise up in my throat.\n\nAnd it's not just the places, it's my friends, too. My hiking buddies... we used to be so tight. We had this group chat where we'd plan all our weekend trips, talk about gear, you know, just normal stuff. But now, I just... I dodge their texts. I leave them on read, or I make up some excuse about picking up extra shifts at work. Because even just texting about going out there, even just seeing a message like, \"Hey, weather's looking good for a hike this weekend,\" it makes my chest tighten up so bad I feel like I can't breathe. My heart starts pounding, and I get that same awful, trapped feeling I had under the rocks. So I just avoid them. I feel terrible about it, because they're good guys and they're probably wondering what's wrong with me, but I just can't handle the emotions that come with even talking about it. So yeah, I try to avoid the feelings, the places, the people... basically anything that reminds me of that day, I just push it as far away as I possibly can.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing before the incident at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, the hiking, obviously, I don't do that anymore, like I said. But everything else? No, I still like doing the things I always liked doing. Like, I'm a big gamer, right? It's a good distraction, you know? I can just put my headset on, get lost in this totally different world for hours, and not have to think about anything else. I'll sit down after a shift at work, and next thing I know, it's 2:00 in the morning and I've just been grinding out quests the whole time. \n\nAnd I still like working on my car. I was the one leading the hikes, the one who wasn't afraid to get close to the edge, the one who acted like nothing could touch me. I had this whole image, I guess. I was tough. I didn't get rattled. And now... now I'm the guy who got trapped under a rockslide because I was stupid enough to stand on an unstable overhang. I should have known better. I grew up around here, I know how the cliffs are after heavy rain, but I just... I didn't think it would happen to me. \n\nAnd now, when I'm at work, dealing with customers or whatever, or even just walking down the street, I have this paranoid feeling that everyone can see it on me. Like they can look at me and just tell that I messed up, that I'm weak, that I'm not the tough guy I always pretended to be. It's like I'm wearing a sign that says \"I made a huge mistake and now I'm terrified all the time.\" It's humiliating. I feel like my friends probably talk about it behind my back, too. Like, \"Oh, look at him now, can't even drive past a trail without hyperventilating.\" I just feel so much shame about the whole thing, like I brought it all on myself and now everyone knows I'm a fraud. It just... it eats at me, you know? Most days, that's just the baseline of how I feel. Scared and totally embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid feeling emotions related to what happened at the bluff?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, absolutely. I mean, I try to avoid anything that even remotely brings up those feelings, because it's just... it's too much. It's not just the feelings, though, it's the places, the people, everything connected to it. Like, I completely refuse to go anywhere near the coast trails now. I used to go out there all the time, right? Like, at least twice a week, rain or shine. But now, just the thought of driving down that stretch of the highway, seeing the ocean on one side and the cliffs on the other... my stomach just drops. I can't do it. I've been taking this ridiculously long detour to get to work just so I don't have to see the water or the bluffs in the distance. It adds like twenty minutes to my commute, but I don't care. It's better than feeling that panic start to rise up in my throat.\n\nAnd it's not just the places, it's my friends, too. My hiking buddies... we used to be so tight. We had this group chat where we'd plan all our weekend trips, talk about gear, you know, just normal stuff. But now, I just... I dodge their texts. I leave them on read, or I make up some excuse about picking up extra shifts at work. My heart starts pounding, and I get that same awful, trapped feeling I had under the rocks. So I just avoid them. It's a good distraction, you know? I can just put my headset on, get lost in this totally different world for hours, and not have to think about anything else. I'll sit down after a shift at work, and next thing I know, it's 2:00 in the morning and I've just been grinding out quests the whole time. \n\nAnd I still like working on my car. Concentrating? No, not really. I mean, I haven't really noticed any issues with that. Like I was saying about the video games, right? I can sit there for literally six, seven hours straight, just totally dialed in. I don't lose focus at all. I'm tracking all these different quests, managing my inventory, coordinating with other players online if I'm doing a raid or something... it takes a lot of mental energy to keep all that straight, and I don't miss a beat. If anything, my focus is almost too intense when I'm doing that stuff, because I just want to block everything else out. 

And at work... well, I do customer service, mostly over the phone and some email support. It's not exactly rocket science, but you do have to pay attention to what the person is saying, look up their account, troubleshoot their issue, log the call... there's a whole process to it. And I haven't messed any of that up. I haven't been getting bad reviews from customers or having my manager pull me aside to say I'm making mistakes. I just put my headset on, pull up the CRM software, and just grind through the queue. I can read through a long, complicated email from a frustrated customer and figure out exactly what's wrong with their order without having to read it three times, you know? 

Even with the car stuff... like, when you're doing brakes, you have to be paying attention. You can't just space out when you're putting the calipers back on or bleeding the lines, because if you screw that up, you're going to crash. And I don't have any problem keeping my mind on the task when I'm out there with my tools. It's like, my hands know what to do, and my brain is right there with them. I'm checking the torque specs, making sure everything is aligned perfectly. So, yeah, I don't think my concentration has taken a hit at all. I'm just as sharp with that stuff as I was before. It's just the emotional stuff that's a wreck, but my actual ability to focus on a task? No, that's totally fine. I haven't noticed any change there whatsoever.
